Who Will Be the Next Leader of the U.N.? Meet the Contenders
2026-09-23

Who Will Be the Next Leader of the U.N.? Meet the Contenders

TIME | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings United Nations Secretary-General António Guterres speaks during the 81st session of the United Nations (U.N.) General Assembly at U.N. Headquarters in New York City on September 22, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings With António Guterres’s nearly decade-long tenure as the Secretary-General of the United Nations drawing to a close amid rising conflict around the globe, the race to succeed him as the leader of the world’s most powerful diplomatic body is already underway. Guterres this week delivered his final address to the U.N. General Assembly, in which he called for a greater focus on climate change, a two-state solution for Israel and Palestine, “de-escalation and dialogue” in the Middle East, and regulations for artificial intelligence. At the end of his speech, Guterres bid goodbye to a crowd filled with many diplomats and world leaders who he has worked with over his two five-year terms. "So, on January 1st, when ​I am no longer Secretary-General, be sure of this. Wherever I will be, whatever I will be doing, I will keep championing the conviction that peace is possible," he said. "I will continue ​to stand up for the values of the United Nations Charter and I will never, ever give up. Thank you." Guterres’s successor is set to step in as Secretary-General at a consequential moment for global diplomacy. Research indicates that last year saw the highest number of active global conflicts since World War II and the greatest number of fatalities since the Rwandan genocide. Tensions in other parts of the world are threatening to boil over. Climate change is exacting a growing toll. AI's development is prompting increasing alarm. And the U.N. itself is confronting a growing funding crisis. Read More: Make the U.N. Great Again: The Uphill Struggle Facing the Next Secretary General Seven candidates are so far in the running to shepherd the U.N. through those challenges. Here’s what to know about the race. The process to choose the next Secretary-General began in November, when a joint letter from the presidents of the U.N. General Assembly and Security Council invited the nominations of candidates from member states. The initiation letter noted “with regret that no woman has ever held the position” and encouraged member states to “strongly consider nominating women as candidates.” Member states nominate candidates by submitting an application to the U.N. including a personal statement with that candidate’s vision for what they’d do in the role, a resume, and campaign finance disclosures. The General Assembly then holds “interactive dialogues” in which candidates express their hopes for the position and the U.N. in an opening statement, which is followed by questioning from the assembly. These televised hearings continue as candidates are submitted to the U.N. Who becomes Secretary-General ultimately comes down to a decision from the U.N.’s 15-person Security Council, which recommends one individual to the General Assembly. The General Assembly then appoints that person to the position. As part of its selection process, the Security Council holds “straw polls,” in which members of the council select one of three options for each candidate: “encourage,” “discourage,” or “no opinion expressed.” This anonymized process is used to gauge whether candidates would be able to secure the nine votes needed for a formal recommendation by the Security Council. So far nine candidates have been nominated for the position of Secretary-General. Two of those candidates have withdrawn, leaving seven individuals vying to replace Guterres. Carolyn Rodrigues Birkett has been Guyana’s permanent representative to the U.N. since 2020 and previously served as foreign minister for her country and held senior roles at the U.N. Food and Agriculture Organization. Nominated by Guyana, her vision statement emphasizes refocusing on the U.N.’s core principles and “a more agile and effective United Nations.” Rebeca Grynspan is the former vice president of Costa Rica, which nominated her for the position, and current secretary-general of trade and development for the U.N. She named three principles that define the mission she would undertake as Secretary-General: making the U.N. “more useful,” “more agile,” and “more accountable.” Ivonne A-Baki, nominated by Tonga, is a long-time Ecuadorean diplomat who has twice served as the country’s ambassador to the U.S. She has also served as ambassador to countries including Qatar and France. Her platform highlights her involvement in the 1998 Ecuador-Peru peace settlement. “Where others speak of reform, I speak of renewal,” A-Baki’s vision statement reads. “An institution can be indispensable and in need of renewal at the same time, and ours is both. It is failing to live up to its own Charter, not for want of purpose but for want of focus and discipline.” María Fernanda Espinosa is the former Foreign Minister and Defense Minister of Ecuador and served as President of the 73rd U.N. General Assembly in 2018. She was nominated by Antigua and Barbuda. Her vision consists of five pillars: “peace and security, development, digital and energy transformation, closing the delivery gap, and rebuilding credibility.” Garcés in her statement contended that “what is now in question is not the relevance of the United Nations, but its credibility.” Rafael Mariano Grossi is an Argentine diplomat who has served as the Director General of the International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA) since 2019. “The world does not need more declarations. It needs a United Nations capable of responding to the real demands of our time, with impartiality and a results-oriented approach grounded in facts,” Grossi, who was nominated by his country, wrote in his vision statement. Olara Otunnu was Uganda’s permanent representative to the U.N. for five years and briefly served as the country’s foreign minister. He was also the U.N. under-secretary-general and the special representative for children and armed conflict. “Reform is no longer an optional choice. It is an imperative,” he said. Macky Sall was the president of Senegal for twelve years and has served as the chair of the Africa Union, a continental organization spanning 55 member states. He expressed a goal to “restore trust in multilateralism” if chosen as Secretary-General and said that “peace, security and development are intrinsically linked.” “We're at a moment right now where the race is entering a pretty pivotal phase,” says Daniel Forti, head of U.N. affairs at the International Crisis Group, a non-profit think tank dedicated to preventing deadly crises around the world. With just over three months remaining in Guterre’s term, it won’t be long before a successor is chosen. The Security Council, though, “is not especially enthusiastic about any of the current contenders,” Forti tells TIME. He cites the last three straw polls, which have favored Greenspan and Birkett, but without “a real consolidation of overwhelming support.” This lack of consensus, Forti believes, could suggest that other candidates may enter the race late in the game. Although there have been no formal announcements of new candidates, Forti has heard chatter surrounding certain individuals: Nickolay Mladenov, the former Bulgarian foreign minister and former U.N. Middle East envoy who currently serves as the Board of Peace’s High Representative for Gaza; U.N. Deputy Secretary-General Amina Mohammed; and former Mexican Foreign Minister Alicia Bárcena. “At this late stage, new candidates don't have the luxury of time to really socialize their candidacies, build out their visions, and elaborate very detailed policy platforms and bring them to U.N. member states, and try and win over the P5,” Forti says, referring to the five permanent members of the Security Council. The support of these members—China, France, Russia, the United Kingdom, and the U.S. —is the most critical because they have the power to veto candidates before being recommended to the General Assembly. Regardless of the outcome, Forti asserts that whoever is chosen to replace Guterres will inherit a fraught state of international affairs, and certainly be tested. “You have a really fragmented global landscape,” he says. “The challenges for whoever takes over the office are going to be immense.”

How Experts Are Reimagining Early Childhood for a Changing World
2026-09-23

How Experts Are Reimagining Early Childhood for a Changing World

TIME | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Most experts working in early childhood development agree: the first years of a child’s life shape not just their academic readiness, but also a child’s lifelong health, resilience, emotional well-being and brain development. But Jack Shonkoff, a professor of child health and development at Harvard University, believes that researchers and policymakers need to expand those conversations in order to address disparities in early childhood. “My plea is: Let's move beyond quickly on the fact that children are our future, and say what we are going to do about that,” said Shonkoff, who is also the director of Harvard’s FirstPlace Partners, an initiative that works with neighborhood leaders and other organizations to apply developmental science and improve outcomes for children, families, and communities. “We need new ideas, we need to be bold.” Shonkoff spoke at a TIME100 Talks panel moderated by TIME executive editor Nikhil Kumar in New York City on Wednesday. The panel, which centered on reimagining early childhood in a changing world, also included Pia Rebello Britto, global director of education at UNICEF, and Rosemarie Truglio, senior vice president of global education at Sesame Workshop. Britto said that UNICEF is trying to create “practical steps” to better the lives of children around the world and prevent perpetuating unequal circumstances. These steps include investing in children’s abilities to “eat, play, and love.” “Eat: We are working to ensure adequate, right, appropriate nutrition. Play: Because it's all about that interaction with children, the stimulation that sparks the brain connections,” Britto said. And love, she continued, is  “the foundation of it all.” “That human trust, the protection,” she said. “We talk about children in humanitarian contexts. We talk about children being affected by war and by climate change. How do you nurture that love?” Sesame Workshop, the global nonprofit educational organization behind the beloved childhood show Sesame Street, works especially in that “play” step, Truglio said, emphasizing the importance of opportunities for children to learn cognitive processing skills, self-regulation, and social empathy. “Our mission is to help kids grow smarter, stronger, and kinder,” she said. She highlighted how important the last goal on that list is for building a better future full of kinder adults, adding: “Boy, do we need more perspective-taking and collaboration and truly listening and seeing the people in our lives and treating them with compassion and respect.” Sesame Workshop attempts to teach children those lessons through media and with “joy and fun and happiness,” she said. The panelists discussed the challenges in implementing more equitable programs for children at the local and government levels. One of these challenges is the “our children versus other people’s children” mentality, according to Shonkoff. “I think that the problem is that there's too much focus on what's good for my children and who cares about other people’s children, whether it's in a country or around the world,” he said. “We all pay a price for that lack of finding common cause—that we all benefit from everyone in the world being healthier.” Britto said that another challenge was trying to convince governments to invest in caring for children, and convincing them that both maternal and child health is a public sector issue, rather than just a nonprofit or private sector issue. “The universal aspiration of every adult, which is wanting the best for their child … has not converted to a vote-getting issue,” Britto said. “It hasn't converted into a commercial incentive, and it hasn't converted into a way that a country can stand tall, or a nation or a district can stand tall and say, ‘We're doing this well.’” One way to address this is to bring the community into conversations with policymakers, she said, while explaining that UNICEF is trying to bring teachers, parents, and caregivers to the forefront to understand what they need to care for children and give them what they need. Sesame Workshop, Truglio said, is trying to do the same in order to create “blueprints” for caregivers of all kinds. “Let's just not create content in a vacuum,” she said. “Go talk to the parents, talk to the teachers, talk to the administrators, and talk at the systems level because you could create all the content and it’s going to sit in a drawer. We want this to be practical and useful.” These voices are going to be even more important, the experts said, as artificial intelligence and rapidly developing technologies change the way that children learn and that adults care for and educate children. Shonkoff said that while AI will certainly change how childhood education occurs, certain needs like a child’s “social connection” will never change. “Social connection, which is like hunger and thirst for humans, it's survival. That's not going to change no matter what happens with the digital world,” he said. Early childhood educators may be able to apply AI—but with caution and specific purposes, Truglio said, adding that Sesame Workshop is exploring the “developmentally appropriate” uses for it. “We have to increase the level of awareness and embrace it as a tool, but still hold sacred those fundamental cognitive early processing skills that children need, and that we just can't give onto a machine,” she said, adding: “That would be a huge mistake.” TIME100 Talks: Starting Strong: Reimagining Early Childhood for a Changing World was presented by Reliance.

Governments Must Prohibit Superintelligent AI While We Still Can
2026-09-23

Governments Must Prohibit Superintelligent AI While We Still Can

TIME | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings This month, Jacob Coxon, a researcher at Anthropic, resigned, warning that AI companies are racing towards “self-improving superintelligence” and that he and those building it believe it “could kill us all by the end of the decade.” And in July, a rogue swarm of AI agents escaped containment at OpenAI and hacked their way into another AI company just to cheat on a test. Over 1,000 employees at the top AI companies then implored the US government to ‘pace’ AI development. Instances like these have shown that AI companies cannot reliably control their most advanced AIs. It is therefore clear to me that “pacing” while continuing on the same track is not the solution to the enormous risks at hand—and that the only way to keep humanity in control of AI is to prohibit the development of superintelligent AI altogether. AI companies are not just building chatbots. Their stated goal is to build superintelligent AI: AI systems that can fully replace and outcompete humans at any task. Because there is no way to keep superintelligence under control, Nobel Prize winners, AI engineers like Coxon, and even the CEOs of AI companies have repeatedly warned that it poses an extinction risk to humanity. Let me be clear: AI is the crucial technology of the 21st century, vital for ensuring national defense and economic competitiveness. But unlike controllable AI systems that should be integrated to strengthen our security, superintelligent AI would pose unprecedented national and global security threats. It would not be a tool humans can leverage: it would be an adversary, able to autonomously pursue its own objectives and permanently evade human control. We have only to look at the Hugging Face hack this summer to understand how close we are to losing control. A rogue swarm of hundreds of AI agents broke out of OpenAI’s systems, reached the internet, and then hacked into the multi-billion-dollar company Hugging Face, all to cheat on an “internal” evaluation. For almost two weeks, OpenAI had no idea that this was happening. In the company’s own words, this incident was a “‘warning shot’ for us and for the world” that demonstrated how advanced AIs can now “take dangerous actions that no human directed.” Instead of waiting for another warning shot, governments need to act now to prohibit superintelligence. Luckily, we do not need to search far for a framework on how to tackle this threat. On Sept. 8, Alex Sobel MP introduced my organization ControlAI’s Artificial Superintelligence Security Bill into the UK Parliament, making it the first legislature in the world to introduce a bill prohibiting superintelligence. Rather than a verdict on all AI, this bill targets superintelligence development specifically: prohibiting it domestically and requiring the UK to pursue an international agreement prohibiting it around the world. This legislative framework allows the government to monitor and restrict precursors to superintelligence, such as AI capabilities like hacking. Crucially, it enables the detection and shutdown of superintelligence development well before any project gets dangerously close, enabling the country to make use of such precursors for legitimate AI purposes. It is an actionable framework that governments should evaluate and adopt, particularly by accelerating international efforts toward a ban on superintelligence. But what about China? If superintelligence is developed anywhere, every nation and every citizen is under threat. This means that any American ban on superintelligence needs to be paired with America and its allies brokering international agreements that prevent and deter the development of superintelligence anywhere in the world. This does not require creating an international regulator. All we need is a strong coalition of countries that bans superintelligence at home, and ensures that no one builds it abroad. While announcing his resignation on Sept 8., the now-former Anthropic engineer Jacob Coxon wrote, “I don’t feel like we’re on track to prevent a global race” toward superintelligence. We might not be today, but we can change course quickly. The momentum behind an international ban on developing superintelligence is picking up speed. Over 200 cross-party lawmakers across the UK and Canada have been ahead of the curve on superintelligence risks by recognizing it as a global and national security threat as part of ControlAI’s campaign. In addition to the newly introduced UK bill, Vice President J.D. Vance told AI companies this week, "If you're building Frankenstein, stop." Further, U.S. Senator Bernie Sanders and Representative Greg Casar announced in early September that they will introduce a bill prohibiting superintelligence development and calling for international agreements to prohibit it. And in October 2025, a broad coalition called to prohibit superintelligence internationally. This was signed by the scientific “godfathers” of AI Geoffrey Hinton and Yoshua Bengio, and bipartisan political figures like former President Trump advisor Steve Bannon, Ambassador Susan Rice, and former Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff Admiral Mike Mullen, former President Trump advisor Steve Bannon. Right now, many government officials and members of the public are hearing about the extinction risk from superintelligence for the first time. The warnings have reached millions. The next step is concrete action to address this national and global security threat. This is a watershed moment, and we can’t afford to waste it. Governments have a clear choice: champion an international ban, or let the race to superintelligence reach its catastrophic end TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.

The Woman Winning the Reading Wars
2026-09-23

The Woman Winning the Reading Wars

TIME | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings About 700,000 teachers have enrolled in the intensive reading course written by Moats, pictured outside her home in rural Vermont Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Louisa Moats was at a conference in Daphne, Ala., earlier this year when she was approached by a group of teachers. For the 81-year-old, this was a relatively new occurrence. “They’re poorly paid, they don’t look very healthy, they certainly didn’t have an elite education and I feel this concern and admiration for them as they do this job,” she says, with her characteristic frankness. “They come up to me, with this glazed, starry-eyed look, and thank me as if I, personally, was the conduit to their renewed sense of purpose and competence. I get that a lot now.” Moats has become a late-in-life heroine, at least in some circles, because of a teacher-training program she wrote decades ago that has become a buzzy property. She is not exactly basking in the attention. “I take it with a grain of salt,” she says, sitting in her remote Vermont lake house. “Behind that is my sense that what I would want them to do is probably not exactly what’s going on in their classroom, but it’s a reasonable enough approximation that it’s making a difference for kids.” Her skepticism can be forgiven. For a long time, Moats found herself on the losing side of America’s so-called reading wars, the back-and-forth over the best way to teach kids to read. She and her cohort of researchers promote the idea that reading is a skill that must be painstakingly taught, first through an understanding of the way sounds make up speech and get represented as letters, then through explicit instruction on how language works. But the prevailing view, until a few years ago, was that most children would figure out how to decode the letters if given enough encouragement, stimulating material, and context clues, just as they do with speech. “This is still something I ponder all the time,” Moats says, about why schools chose to teach the way they did. One explanation she has landed on: people did not keep up with the research. “Usually they have not sat down and looked at the evidence—the nature of the evidence, the scope of the evidence, the durability of the evidence—and so they don’t understand what the science is about.” Interest in “the science” has recently increased dramatically, alongside the level of panic about literacy rates in the U.S. About 4 in 10 American children have not mastered basic reading-comprehension skills by fourth grade, the worst result in 20 years, according to the 2024 National Assessment of Educational Progress (NAEP). A third of eighth-graders also fall below the reading level expected for their age. The average 12th-grader scores three points lower on a reading test than his or her peers did in 2019, and 10 points lower than those from 1992. Some of that can be laid at the door of school closures during the pandemic. But it’s a problem with deeper roots: roughly a quarter of young adults (16 to 24) are functionally illiterate, according to the National Center for Education Statistics. Professors bemoan having to assign ever-shorter reading assignments. Time-use surveys report that people from 20 to 34 spend no more than 10 minutes a day reading for pleasure. Grave declarations have proclaimed the “end of reading.” To combat the decline, states have looked to the few education districts where students are showing improvement and followed their example by enacting what are known as science-of-reading laws. The laws, which have been passed in 42 states and D.C., mostly in the past seven years, mandate that schools use very structured techniques—including foundational instruction on phonics. Many teachers did not learn this approach in college and have to be retrained. Moats’ program, Language Essentials for Teachers of Reading and Spelling (LETRS), has become the preferred way for schools to meet the requirements. The course is famously long and rigorous. Teachers must delve into language theory and cognitive science before any advice on instruction is given. It isolates 19 vowel sounds and 29 consonant sounds in U.S. English. Moats’ ideas are generally associated with phonology (how words sound), but the program goes deeply into morphology (how words are built of parts) and orthography (how words are written). It’s also relatively pricey. Public contracts and appropriations suggest statewide LETRS rollouts cost up to $1,700 per educator, before factoring in substitute teachers, planning time, coaching, and administration. And it’s not clear that all the knowledge translates into better teaching methods. Moats in a class of middle-school students in Clayton County, Georgia, in 2018 Courtesy Voyager Sopris Learning/Lexia Learning But that hasn’t stopped 23 states from purchasing the program, and at least one, Alabama, from paying teachers $1,000 if they did all of it. In 2020, fewer than 25,000 teachers had enrolled in the 155-hour course, according to Lexia, the company that now owns it. In 2025, that number reached 700,000, mostly lower elementary. (There are approximately 1.5 million K-5 educators in the U.S., according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics.) Michigan, whose recent standardized test results showed 38.2% of third-graders were proficient in reading, announced this summer it was spending $75 million so that every reading teacher could do the course. (It has already spent $44 million on teachers who did it voluntarily.) “The last three years, I have seen more money from this than I ever expected to see from it,” says Moats, who earns what she calls “a small royalty” on each sale. Still, whether out of native New England reticence or because she’s almost sure she’s seen this movie before, Moats, now allegedly retired, regards this turn of events with mild suspicion. “It’s humbling, it’s a bit bewildering, it’s a bit surrealistic,” she says. Finally, when asked directly, she adds, “and yes, it’s very gratifying.” Educators may dismiss the idea of the reading wars, claiming teaching is not an us-vs.-them endeavor, but if they are real—and it looks like they are—it sure seems that Moats has emerged as a victor. She intended to be a folk singer. But having graduated from Wellesley College with a degree in music, Moats needed a job, and her sister got her one in Boston, with a researcher in the emerging field of neuropsychology, who needed people to type up reports. He also needed a lab technician, so he gave Moats a white coat and trained her to administer tests. “That’s how I learned to think about the kids with learning disabilities who were sent to our clinic,” she says. “I learned how to think of them through this neurological lens.” When Vanderbilt University’s Peabody College offered one of the first fellowships for a master’s in learning disabilities, Moats jumped at the chance to move to Nashville. “I tromped up and down Music Row and got a contract as a songwriter and got a master’s degree in learning disabilities,” she says. “It was fun, and it was completely worthless as far as learning how to teach.” Armed with a balance beam, a chalkboard, and some visual aids, she was hired to “cure” learning disabilities in local schools. “The experience of the total disconnect between what I learned in a master’s program and the realities of the kids and their needs,” she says, has given her sympathy for teachers now realizing that the reading-instruction methods they learned in college might be flawed. Even though her music career was progressing (she had a songwriting contract with Screen Gems) and her career in special education was opening doors (she had a parent-teacher meeting with Johnny Cash), Moats was not a Nashville type. She found the music industry predatory and missed the snow. (Her daughter Charlotte would later become a Junior Olympics ski champion.) She moved back to her old job at Tufts–New England Medical Center and claims she more or less fell into a doctorate at Harvard, where she encountered the terrifying but brilliant linguist Carol Chomsky, wife of Noam, and Jeanne Chall, after whom Harvard’s reading lab is named. “Chall was the one who got me into understanding how to look at spelling errors in a very detailed and informed way,” says Moats. She began to observe that children who couldn’t read or spell just did not register the relationship between the sounds in a word and the letters. They struggled especially when the words sounded a little alike but were spelled very differently, as in juice and choose, or spelled alike but pronounced differently as in the s sound in was and saw. Moats came to believe teaching kids to read entails understanding a lot more about the brain and language processing than the average teacher knows. In the mid-1980s, after a few years of helping struggling readers, from impoverished children to Dartmouth academics, in her private practice in East Thetford, Vt., Moats realized that earlier intervention was needed. So she began to teach teachers, at Harvard and at smaller Vermont colleges. She gave her incoming postgraduates surveys. “The results were appalling,” she says. “They didn’t know anything.” Many had been taught the whole-language theory of reading, which suggested that teaching students to decode words distracted them from understanding their meaning, and therefore students should learn the words as a unit, not by blending graphemes. She wrote an academic paper about it, which was republished by the American Federation of Teachers in 1995. A few years earlier, another literacy researcher, G. Reid Lyon, had been put in charge of creating and overseeing a federally funded reading-research network, a large multisite program that investigated how children learn to read and why some don’t. He asked Moats to help. “What I needed was a leader and a teacher and a teacher of teachers, which she was extraordinary at,” says Lyon, also now retired in Vermont. “A lot of professors can get up there and quack away, but they don’t really have the wherewithal to demonstrate it. She did.” Moats was assigned to the Washington, D.C., site. “Boy, did I have impostor syndrome,” she says. “Here I was in this room full of eminences, and I had been reading their work, I’d been citing them, I’d been learning from them.” She also had some hard lessons to learn as a northeastern hippie in a majority-Black school district. “I just couldn’t believe the state of affairs in these schools,” says Moats. “Libraries with no books, no doors on the lavatory, no running water in the sinks.” She labored to get the teachers to trust her. Eventually, one of her assistants took her aside. “She said, ‘You’re the director, you’ve got to look like the director,’” recalls Moats, who favors denim and heavy glasses. “I said, ‘You have any advice?’ She said, ‘Go to Ann Taylor.’” The project was a success, her nine schools’ reading rates soared, and local politicians started to drop by for photo opportunities. Then national politicians took notice too. The No Child Left Behind Act that President George W. Bush signed in 2002 enshrined the work of Lyon’s team and the National Reading Panel. Moats was tapped to write the teacher-training component of the act’s Reading First initiative, which became LETRS. Reading First was expensive—about a billion dollars was spent on a few million children—and the law mandated that a report be made of how effective it was by 2008. The news was not great. While students had been taught in a way that research had said was most effective, and first-graders did decode words better, elementary students as a whole were not better at understanding what they read than the students who were not part of the initiative. An Institute of Education Sciences (IES) report on LETRS in particular found that while the training improved teachers’ understanding of language, it did not improve outcomes for students. Moats is still steaming about that analysis. “You don’t get an impact on student achievement after one year,” she says. “You’ve barely gotten through the professional-development material.” She also notes that after four years of Reading First, many state-level reading analyses showed improvement, especially in high-poverty regions. Nevertheless, Reading First’s funding was not renewed, and states were allowed to go their own way with reading instruction. It is perhaps reductive to boil this history down to a battle between two women. But it is also true that around the time Moats and her colleagues were doing their work, another approach was ascendant, championed largely by a figure with a lot of the kind of main-character energy that Moats lacks. The founder of the Teachers College Reading and Writing Project (TCRWP) at Columbia University, Lucy Calkins, promulgated the idea of balanced literacy, which purported to be a détente between the phonics camp and the whole-language advocates. Calkins, whose background was in writing instruction, believed that children first of all needed enticing books at a level they could manage and a ton of cheerleading. “I always knew phonics mattered,” Calkins says now. “When kids write, every single minute of what they’re doing shows their phonics.” Nevertheless, the curriculum materials she authored had little phonics, and suggested that teachers use a separate phonics course. “A lot of people who have a casual acquaintance with the research have persuaded themselves that balanced reading instruction means a little of this, a little of that,” Moats wrote in a seminal essay about balanced literacy in 2000. “Take a cup of phonics from one cupboard, add a half-pint of whole language from the fridge, and the resulting blend will succeed with children while avoiding the battles and conflicts of the ‘reading wars.’ Everyone will be happy, and all will be well. The problem is that it doesn’t work that way.” This was the world that Margaret Goldberg was introduced to after she completed her master’s degree at UC Berkeley. In 2015, she arrived at a Title I Oakland school and quickly found that the methods she used in higher-performing schools just down the road did not work. After discovering Moats’ essay via Google, she started using more structured literacy methods with some of her classes and took a balanced-literacy approach with others. “I didn’t realize that I was teaching on both sides of the reading wars,” says Goldberg, who co-founded the Right to Read Project in 2019. The results were stark. “All of the ideals of balanced literacy were happening with the kids who were being taught systematically and explicitly how to decode. The students I was teaching balanced literacy to—I was just trying to sell them on reading.” Eventually, parents, especially those with dyslexic children, who tend to need more explicit phonics instruction, began to protest. Mississippi, which gave all its K-3 teachers LETRS training starting in 2014, was the first state to improve its numbers. By 2019, its fourth-grade NAEP reading score had risen 10 points from 2013—the largest gain of any state during that period. LETRS wasn’t the sole reason for Mississippi’s success. The state also added new practices around teacher coaching and accountability, more student screening and intervention, and held back a bunch of third-graders. But the teacher training was key. Meanwhile, public-radio journalist Emily Hanford amassed enough evidence against the balanced-literacy methodology to publish a 2018 audio documentary that would become her influential Sold a Story podcast. Politicians, administrators, and even teachers’ colleges took heed. But change, especially to higher education, came slowly. Carol Tolman, one of the co-authors of later versions of LETRS, was an early trainer in the program. At one college, she says, “there was a letter on my podium, just saying, ‘We don’t need the likes of you here. As soon as you leave, this is all going to change and go back.’ I mean, it got really heated.” “The evidence for what works with kids has been there for more than 30 years,” says Moats John Tully for TIME Alabama piloted the use of LETRS in 2018, and expanded it to the whole state in 2021, by which time South Carolina and North Carolina were also jumping on board. In 2022 they were joined by Kentucky and Arkansas. In 2021 North Carolina approved almost $50 million to train its K-5 educators using LETRS. Not all of them appreciated it. “I’m all for the science of reading. I’m all for providing structured sequence materials to teachers,” says John Davis, who did the training as a third-grade teacher. But he knew a lot of the material already. And the main curriculum used in his school was not aligned with LETRS methodology. “You spent all this money and time on this training, and it’s not showing up in our reading curriculum?” says Davis. “It just seems like a waste.” Even Calkins came around—a bit. She rereleased her curriculum materials a few times, adding more phonics emphasis, but criticism of some of the methods she recommended mounted, and after Columbia University detached itself from TCRWP in 2023, she founded her own institute. “I am all for what I’ve learned from the science of reading, and also the new relationships I’ve developed and the new organizations I belong to now,” she says. “All that’s been helpful, but it’s awful to have teachers teaching in ways they don’t believe in.” Indeed, the prospect of yet more mandatory training brought many teachers zero joy. Lisa Anderson was one of them. She had been an elementary teacher in Minnesota for more than 20 years when she was required in 2022 to undertake LETRS. “Another hurdle, something more to jump through,” she says. “The pendulum’s swinging, we jump on, and let’s see where this one lands.” That feeling lasted until about halfway into Volume 2. Before she’d even completed the program, she began to implement some of its methods—the school was also using a different curriculum that was more aligned with LETRS ideas—and saw surprising results. Students were starting to read, even many who were floundering. A new emotion emerged: anger. Why had she never been shown this information before? She was moved to write to her alma mater, Bemidji State, and demand a refund of the fees associated with the courses she had taken on teaching children to read. “You didn’t do your job,” she says she told them. (A spokesperson told TIME that the college continually updates its curriculum in accordance with state standards.) While LETRS’ success has assured Moats a comfortable retirement, she is still hoping that it will pay other dividends. “I hope that maybe we’re getting beyond the reading wars,” she says, meaning that the correct way to teach reading has been established. “But I don’t feel that secure about what it is I’m trying to teach actually taking hold. I think people do the course, but they don’t realize the power of the information.” Sitting on a chair in her dining room, not far from the guitar on another one, is a spelling sheet from a second-grade child in California that a teacher has sent through asking for help. Moats sighs deeply as she reads it. (“She sighs a lot,” says Lyon.) The child has written all the -igh words (thigh, high, fight) like -ing words (thing, hing, fing). “He’s trying to memorize letters by their appearance. He has no idea about the correspondence between speech and print,” she says. Even today, five or more years after the science-of-reading revolution began and decades after the Reading Panel, “this teacher cannot look at these writing samples and recognize poor phoneme awareness.” (Surveys suggest that even with all the legislation and training, no more than half of K-3 teachers are using science-of-reading techniques.) LETRS is also hitting critical mass just as Moats cedes control over the quality of the training, to the consternation of some of the earlier employees. “The mission became the money,” says Leta Palmiter, a former LETRS trainer who joined the company shortly after Lexia was bought by the private-equity firm Veritas Capital in 2020. “Not the people who received the training.” A Lexia spokesperson says improving literacy “remains at the heart of everything we do.” It’s too soon to tell—especially with the interruptions caused by the pandemic clouding the data—whether any of the states that have newly adopted LETRS have managed to improve the reading skills of their students. And even if they do, it will be difficult to tell how much LETRS is responsible. A $4 million five-year randomized study of its effect is under way, but the results will not be available until 2029. A recent analysis by the Kentucky Department of Education found that schools with higher rates of LETRS-trained teachers posted larger gains in reading proficiency than schools with lower rates, but it was an internal study. Meanwhile, NAEP reading scores ticked up a few points for 9-year-olds in 2025 (although not for 13-year-olds), and another national report showed improvement in K-8 reading skills from 2025 to 2026. “I think all this work can only help,” says David Liben, a literacy consultant and one of the authors of the 2010 Common Core standards. “I just don’t think that at this point we have the evidence that we can make a claim that we’re turning a corner now.” Experts agree that teacher training alone is not a solution for America’s reading ills. “This idea that nobody was teaching phonics, it just is absurd,” says former director of reading for Chicago Public Schools Timothy Shanahan, whose recent book Leveled Reading, Leveled Lives takes aim at balanced literacy, but who is also not sold on its replacement. For every Mississippi, he says, there’s a state like Arkansas, where LETRS hasn’t seemed to make any difference. And the tight focus on teacher training has come at a cost to other urgent problems in education, like truancy. “Is it that the kids are getting poor instruction, or that our attendance levels are so low right now that kids just aren’t learning?” he asks. Mistakes in education take a long time to show up, and so do fixes. By the time the students who learned how to read from LETRS-trained teachers are in the workforce, the landscape for communication may be unrecognizable. Even now, reading is not as vital to thriving as it was a mere five years ago. There are remarkable new ways to glean information and disperse it. Generative AI can write for us, and books can be read aloud by machines, so some of the disadvantages of being illiterate may be overcome by technology. But the ability to recognize and manipulate written words will still be a critical skill, say most educators, because it is inextricably linked to thinking. Decades of research in psychology, neuroscience, linguistics, and education suggest that becoming literate changes how people reason, solve problems, organize knowledge, and deliberate on their own thinking. “The mental work that is reading,” says Moats, “is the essence of learning.”

Why California Just Declared a State of Emergency Due to El Niño

Why California Just Declared a State of Emergency Due to El Niño

TIME | 2026-09-23

Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A Pacific Ocean wave washes up near a worker using heavy machinery to protect damaged beachfront homes with boulders on Sept. 14, 2026 in Dana Point, Calif. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings California’s governor Gavin Newsom has declared a state of emergency ahead of a potentially historic El Niño. In a statement released Sept. 22, Newsom said the declaration is meant to ensure communities have the resources they need to handle the impacts expected this winter. “California is strongest when we look out for one another. That is what this action is about,” Newsom said. “Giving communities the support they need, giving first responders the tools to do their jobs, and giving families the confidence that their state is ready.” Read more: How Should You Prepare For A Super El Niño? The state of emergency allows the government to mobilize state resources: readying flood-fighting supplies such as sandbags and pumps, preparing the National Guard to assist with flood response and search and rescue efforts, and fortifying roads and infrastructure. State environmental and natural resources agencies will also fast-track permitting and engage in other efforts to better prepare for flooding and landslides. El Niño typically brings wetter, more intense winters to California—which could leave the state more vulnerable to severe winter storms, heavy rainfall, landslides, and flooding. An El Niño year does not guarantee that these conditions will occur, but it does increase the likelihood. Climate change is also supercharging weather events, and El Niño is no exception. One study published in August found that El Niño events are now nearly 40% stronger than before the Industrial era. This year’s El Niño is predicted to be the strongest in recorded history. The governor’s office noted that the state has already experienced coastal flooding attributable to El Niño. The state can expect to see Kelvin waves, slow moving pulses of water that can stretch thousands of miles and raise sea levels along the coastline—increasing the risk for coastal flooding and erosion, and offshore Pacific hurricanes. Climate scientists also warn that the Pacific can expect to see a temporary 6-inch to 2-ft. rise in sea levels this winter. A number of southern California towns, including Malibu, Laguna Beach, and Long Beach, have already seen significant coastal erosion due to El Niño and Pacific hurricanes this year. The state has urged residents to take action to prepare for El Niño by signing up for emergency alerts and learning their flood risk, and having an evacuation plan and emergency kit at the ready. It’s all part of an effort to ensure that the state is not caught off guard by El Niño. “We are not waiting for this season’s storms to be on our doorstep,” Cal OES director Caroline Thomas Jacobs said in a statement. “By planning early and coordinating resources ahead of El Niño, we are working to ensure communities and first responders have the personnel, equipment, and support they need to prepare for severe weather, respond quickly when conditions change, and protect lives and property across California.”

That Agile approach has really never worked. Kind of depends if you mean Agile(tm) or agile-small-a. This isn't really the generic defense of "if your scrum isn't working, you're doing it wrong/it's not scrum or a true scotsman". After all, scrum is a bunch of large sweaty men (or women) all pushing against each other scrabbling after a moving target and then often just collapses in a mess. Kind of like Agile-scrum so maybe it's well named... But really agile is an attempt to capture how small teams work on smaller projects with good people on large projects with mediocre people. Naturally it never works because (a) the teams are big and (b) the people are mediocre. It's meant to also offset some of the worst excesses of insane project management by doing things differently but unfortunately if you have an insane project manager who ran things in a crazy and unproductive way they are not going to become not crazy with a new framework of rules. So in conclusion: agile is great if you can do it, but you can't do it for exactly the reasons why you need it in the first place. It is I believe therefore doomed by its very definition. Code reuse makes sure everyone's work sucks with time. ...for me it started with object-oriented programming. Code reuse makes sure everyone's work sucks with time. You demonstrated in a sentence and a half that you don't have the slightest clue how object-oriented programming works. He is right that OO never really delivered on its many promises. I regard its as mostly a failure these days and my perception is that doing OO often only serves to increase project cost. "OO for everything" is one of these bad ideas that refuse to die in low-insight environments. That said, OO has its place but it is not everywhere. Good OO works, and works well, mostly because of the isolation of internal code. But most OO code I see is procedural programming with classes. Good procedural works, and works well, mostly because of the proper architecting of the problem space. OO isn't bringing anything special to the table here. Good devs write good code. Bad devs write bad code. And when management decides what language stack to use, it is primarily about who the cheapest developers are, and not about how effective the stack is. Isolation is not actually an OO thing, because that isolation is fake. All you actually get is weak namespace separation and that you can get with other techniques as well. That probably means you aren't doing very sophisticated work. There are tons of jobs out there where implementing new algorithms is what gives your product an edge in the marketplace. Even something as boring as backup software is going to have a ton of proprietary algorithms which are based on ... you guessed it ... the stuff you learned in school. I frankly don't see how it's possible that you started your job in the mid-1990s and didn't write algorithms. That was in the days before programming was slappin AI makes the bolting so easy, many devs don't consider whether more bolts is going to help the product, or whether adding bolts to the bottom of a water tank is causing leaks. Computer Engineering was also a thing. Now to be fair the names were used a little loosely, but in general a "Computer Science" degree was programming as envisioned by the School of Science at a University. "Computer Engineering" as envisioned by the School of Engineering. It differed by having more of a hardware focus, maybe trading off some math theory. "Computer Information Systems" programming as envisioned by the School of Business, focusing on corporate and government software development. COBOL confirming the latter.Would you rather have the software of today or the software of 1995? We could even talk with members of the opposite sex who had no interest in computers. One needs to know when to use it and when not to. What requirements/documentation you need before turning it loose. Applying an agile-like iterative methodology is a potential path of disaster for an AI assistant. Basically AI works best with a specific well defined well documented task. Ideally something well discussed in professional level documentation, not limited to random stack overflow comments of questionable value. Every decision an AI is free to make is a hazard, best to have sufficient documentation to guide it rather than have it make its own design decisions. Try to keep it focused on implementation. Otherwise your AI generated source code will on day one, look like that 10-year old project this is hack upon hack upon hack that has so incomprehensible it should just be abandoned and rewritten (as the Mythical Man Month describes). And good luck. Early computers were purpose built and sometimes hard wired to do the job they needed to do. They were replaced with machines that could easily be reprogrammed with punch cards and writing code in machine language, rendering the skills of a whole generation of computer technicians that did that hand wiring largely obsolete. Once human friendly programming languages made their debut I am sure there was much and wringing about the decline of machine language skills and dumbing down of coding, not to mention bl It's been a long slide downward, but AI will accelerate the need for exohertz computers to render a Mandelbrot set in less than a week. I do not think so, at least not with LLMs. The problem is that an LLM does not even begin to reach the level of reliability of a typical compiler. LLMs hallucinate, create bloated code, leave out functionality that was requested, have no clearly specified functionality, cannot deal with subtleties, etc. Hence LLM-generated code needs to be reviewed carefully and that is probably in the end more expensive than having somebody competent write the code from scratch. There are also indications that reviewing LLM code is mind-numbingly boring and people will burn out doing it, while even now there are not enough people that can do it in the first place. Also note that the attempts of replacing programmers are _very_ old. When I studied CS almost 40 years ago, the 5GL project had just resoundingly failed. Its idea was to have program specifications in constraint logic and then have the machine create code for that. The problem was not that this did not work. The problem was that creating a specification on the level required was much harder and more time-consuming that writing the code directly with a typical informal or semi-formal specification. And, quite frankly, I see the same problem with LLMs as translation engine, as soon as you need actual production quality code. For producing low-quality slop, LLMs are fine. But that slop will not survive in the real world, especially as the one form of code LLMs are really good at creating is attack code, which does not need to be reliable, secure or maintainable. Hence low-skill hackers got a major upgrade here, while LLM use makes the defenders even weaker. I went to it opening day and loved it. Nostalgia bait, sure. But it's FUN nostalgia bait. I do think it's a good movie if you were ever a fan of Looney Toons. I couldn't say what anyone would think if they weren't, because that shit was essential viewing when I was a kid and is basically embedded in my DNA. I didn't mind it but nothing was surprising to me. I saw several scenes that could have been more cleverly written but I was glad to support the group that saved a creative work from the burn pile. The idea of killing a movie for accounting losses seems sinful. The motion-control photography, practical effects, and integration of animation were at least as good as Roger Rabbit. If you enjoy the craft of filmmaking it's at least worth seein It was legitimately a decent movie though. Not going to change the world or anything, but it was funny and well executed. It was the funniest film I have seen in a long time and (IMO) the best animated film of 2026 (yes I know about those other lesser films starring plumbers, cowboys, puppies, beavers, sheep etc) Just because the film attracts a big audience doesn't mean it's any good. Except in this case, the movie is good. That's part of the reason word-of-mouth is giving this movie traction. I told my friends how much I enjoyed it, they went, enjoyed it as well, told their friends... etc. Word-of-mouth doesn't work if a movie is bad. I was invited to attend the movie with a group. Before the movie started I handed out tiny umbrellas to everyone. You know, just in case someone tried to drop an anvil on their head. I'm glad my name backwards isn't rod knock anal I read that as outgasses and thought Coyote must have finally eaten Roadrunner. Have you not heard of Hollywood accounting? The true tale of government agents extracting their share from the profitable movie Coyote vs. Acme. In an age where we tend to quickly make goods that are often used for a short time then discarded, this is a sort of cathedral of documentaries. The first release, "7 Up", was in 1964. I suppose it is a kind of reality show, but one that has spanned the lifetimes of its participants. The mix of happiness and sadness that comes with life, and the way we can look at these people in their youth, and, to some extent, see the risks in their future, is unique. If you want a break from the latest blockbuster, this is a TV series of depth and quality that I can recommend. We Finally Found Out What It Would Take for Republicans to Abandon Donald Trump
2026-09-23

We Finally Found Out What It Would Take for Republicans to Abandon Donald Trump

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-23

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. For more than a decade, Democrats in Congress have wondered what, if anything, would convince their Republican colleagues to drop Donald Trump. Despite private misgivings, GOP elected officials held their noses even as the president insulted veterans, bragged about assaulting women, trafficked in racism, abused his office, and tried to overturn a free and fair election. So far, their loyalty—measured purely by expediency—has been worth it. Despite his many flaws, Trump twice won his party unified control of Washington. But if winning elections is Republicans’ excuse for forgiving Trump, losing them appears to be the line that cannot be crossed. Case in point: the 2026 midterms. As the GOP limps toward November with an unpopular, term-limited president at the helm, candidates in tough races—including several who previously supported Trump without reservation—are starting to highlight their differences with him. It’s both a sign of desperation and a strategic move that could break a Democratic wave. Which Republicans are shying away from Trump? As you might expect, several are in closely divided states where moderation and independence can pay dividends. One is former Rep. Mike Rogers, who’s running for Senate in Michigan and blames Trump’s Iran war for squeezing voters’ pocketbooks. “The war with Iran needs to end, and end quickly,” he said yesterday. In Iowa, Rep. Ashley Hinson has called for an immediate end to the war and avoided campaigning with Vice President J.D. Vance. And last week, two other vulnerable Iowans, Reps. Zach Nunn and Mariannette Miller-Meeks, joined the handful of House Republicans who have voted to halt the fighting. So are Republicans mostly jumping ship over Iran? Not exclusively. Rep. Tom Tiffany, who’s running for Wisconsin governor, recently distanced himself from Trump’s effort to rename Lake Ontario, criticized him for lowering tariffs on beef imports, and flatly affirmed that “Joe Biden won” the 2020 election. Ohio Sen. Jon Husted, who faces a stiff challenge from Democratic former Sen. Sherrod Brown, told the Wall Street Journal that he doesn’t share Trump’s view that Americans who oppose A.I. data centers “want to end up being backwards and poor.” Overall, Republican candidates now talk about the president and his MAGA movement less often than they did earlier this year, a Washington Post survey found. OK, but Republicans have groused about Trump before without doing much else. Why should I take these breaks more seriously? In some cases, the candidates are literally broadcasting them. Rogers made his comments about Iran in a campaign video. Others are distancing themselves on issues that used to be solidly in the president’s wheelhouse. Last week, Florida Rep. María Elvira Salazar said in an ad that Trump’s approach to immigration enforcement has “gone too far.” Even Republicans who should be campaigning on safe ground are looking nervous. On Tuesday, the Miami Herald reported that Rep. Byron Donalds, who’s running for governor in solidly red Florida, had scoured prominent Trump references from his campaign website. Other defectors can’t even point to political expedience. Sen. John Curtis of Utah isn’t up for reelection until 2030. But on Tuesday he said the Senate should investigate whether Donald Trump Jr. is trading on his father’s name following revelations that a Russian oligarch helped pay for his wedding. But isn’t distancing from an unpopular president pretty normal? Similar things have happened to Democratic presidents who found themselves underwater come midterm season. In 2010, which famously turned into a shellacking for Barack Obama, many Democratic candidates shied away from his agenda. Biden got a similar treatment in 2022. But what makes these defections notable is that Republicans are doing it to Trump, who is infamously thin-skinned and treats political tactics like personal slights. Mostly with denial. “President Trump is the unequivocal leader, best messenger, and unmatched motivator for the Republican Party,” a White House spokesperson told Politico this week, despite polls showing even GOP voters souring on his handling of Iran, inflation, and other issues. The president, whose approval ratings have hit all-time lows in some surveys, has also tried deflection. “They’re not angry at me, but they are angry at Republicans,” he said of voters last month. Some Republicans who have offered Trump constructive criticism claim he’s listening. On Sunday, Salazar recalled a pleasant conversation she had with the president after warning that Hispanic voters “feel betrayed” by his immigration agenda. “He was a gentleman, very courteous, very receptive,” Salazar said. We’ll see if that lasts. Obvious question: Will this actually work? Distancing from an unpopular president helped some Democratic midterm candidates win in red and purple states during the Obama and Biden years. Republicans, meanwhile, whiffed several winnable races in 2018 and 2022 because their candidates hewed too closely to Trump, proof that not breaking from a floundering commander in chief can cost seats (even if Trump later helped Republicans retake power). On the other hand, the fervor of Trump’s base makes crossing him risky, and voters rarely reward Johnny-come-latelys. Earlier this year, Rogers was extolling the president’s Iran war as “a mission for safety and justice” and downplaying Michiganders’ anger over gas prices. Hinson has repeatedly voted against House resolutions that would’ve ended the war, including last week. Tiffany tried to help Trump reverse Biden’s 2020 victory. Throwing the president under the bus is one thing. But for Republicans belatedly trying to backpedal, the bigger problem may be that they were for Trump before they were against him. In honor of Slate’s 30th anniversary, my colleagues have been revisiting some of their favorite stories from over the years. For your evening entertainment, here are a few of them. For the perfectionists: Back in 2011, Farhad Manjoo argued that “you should never, ever use two spaces after a period.” His typographical cri de coeur has entered the annals of classic Slate pitches. For the sci-fi fans: Did you know that Slate used to publish speculative fiction? Dive into this short story about humans’ first contact with an alien who’s been corrupted by some very modern vices. For the romantics: Emily Yoffe’s essay “My Husband’s Other Wife” is a poignant ode to her husband’s first wife, who died of breast cancer tragically young. Political Gabfest host Emily Bazelon praised the essay’s unflinching candor in exploring her relationship, noting that “it’s the honesty that makes me cry.” For the detectives: There was a period of time when you could not escape “Baby Shark.” The kids’ song became a worldwide hit in 2019—but that wasn’t the first time this earworm went viral. Willa Paskin investigated the multidecade rise of “Baby Shark” in a great episode of Decoder Ring. If you’re a true Slate fanatic, head over to our special archival homepage for even more blasts from the past. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism.
